Can you tell me what’s been troubling you or what brings you here today?\n\nPatient: I came in because my mind keeps… going blank, I guess is the best way to put it. It’s not just forgetting little things like where I put my keys. It happens at the worst times, when I’m supposed to know exactly what I’m doing.\n\nLike with work, I’ll be looking right at the road-closure forms, or holding the stop-slow paddle, something I’ve done for years, and suddenly it’s like the steps disappear out of my head. I know I should know what comes next, and I’m standing there trying to pull it back, but there’s just this blank space. It scares me because those are not things you can be fuzzy about, you know? There are cars, people, instructions, routines… and I’ve done them so many times, but then my brain just doesn’t give me the next step.\n\nThat’s really what pushed me to come in. It feels like my thinking is not working when I need it most.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any other symptoms along with these blank episodes, like numbness, weakness, trouble speaking, or seeing something unusual like flashing lights or blind spots?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way I could clearly point to. I mean, when it happens I get really scared and kind of frozen because I’m trying so hard to remember what I’m supposed to do next, but I haven’t noticed, like, one side of my body going numb or weak or anything like that.\n\nTrouble speaking… I don’t know. Sometimes I probably get quiet because I’m embarrassed or panicking internally, but I don’t think my mouth stops working exactly. It’s more like my thoughts won’t line up fast enough for me to say anything useful.\n\nAnd seeing things like flashing lights or blind spots—no, I haven’t noticed that. Not that I can remember. It’s more just this blankness, like the instructions or the next step vanish, and then I’m standing there trying to act normal while my brain catches up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you briefly lost awareness of what was going on around you during one of these blank episodes, even for just a few seconds?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. That’s the hard part, because from inside it doesn’t feel like I black out exactly. I don’t come to and find myself somewhere else, or have someone telling me I was gone for a while. It’s more like… I’m there, I can see what’s in front of me, but I’m not taking it in the way I should.\n\nLike I might be staring at the sign or the paperwork or the road, and for a few seconds everything feels sort of paused in my head. I know people are around, cars are there, things are still happening, but my brain isn’t connecting the meaning of it fast enough. So I don’t know if that counts as losing awareness. It doesn’t feel like being unconscious. It feels more like I’m present but not really processing, if that makes sense.\n\nI haven’t noticed a clear gap where time is missing, but I also worry that maybe I wouldn’t know if it was only a few seconds.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had trouble remembering what happened right before or after one of these blank episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it trouble remembering what happened right before or right after, exactly. It’s more like the part I can’t remember is what I’m supposed to be doing in the moment.\n\nLike, I can usually tell you, “I was looking at the road-closure forms,” or “I had the stop-slow paddle in my hand,” or “I was standing there getting ready to do the next part.” That part is still there. But then the actual steps — the routine I’ve done for years — just drop out of my head. It’s like I’m staring right at the thing that should remind me, and it still doesn’t connect.\n\nAfterward, I can remember that it happened, and I remember feeling scared and embarrassed because I know I should know what comes next. But in the middle of it, it’s just blank. Not like a missing chunk of time so much as my mind not giving me the instructions I need.\n\nDoctor: During these blank episodes, has there ever been a moment where you felt like you couldn’t respond or move, even though you were aware of what was going on around you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, but it’s hard to describe because it doesn’t feel like my body is paralyzed or anything. It’s more like my mind just stops giving me the next instruction, and then I freeze because I don’t know what to do.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in, because it keeps happening at the worst possible times. I’ll be looking right at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and these are things I’ve done for years, things I should know without even thinking. And then suddenly it’s just… gone. The steps aren’t there. I know I’m standing there, I know the road is there, I know I’m supposed to be doing something, but I can’t pull the thought together fast enough to respond normally.\n\nSo if someone asked me something in that moment, I might not answer right away, not because I don’t hear them, but because I’m trying to make my brain catch up. And moving is kind of the same — I can move, I think, but I hesitate or just stand there because I don’t trust what the next move is supposed to be. It scares me because it’s not some complicated new thing. It’s basic steps I’ve done over and over, and then my mind just blanks out on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any shaking, jerking, or other movements that you couldn’t control during or right after one of these blank episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, like jerking or shaking that I couldn’t stop. I haven’t noticed my arms or legs doing anything on their own, and nobody has told me, “Hey, you were shaking,” or anything like that.\n\nWhen it happens, from what I can tell, I’m more just stuck or frozen for a moment, like I’m standing there trying to get my brain to catch up. Afterward I might feel rattled or embarrassed, but I can’t say I’ve had any obvious movements I couldn’t control. If there was something really subtle, I might not know, but nothing that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: During these blank episodes, have you ever noticed yourself making any automatic or repetitive movements, like lip-smacking, fumbling with your hands, or doing something over and over without realizing it?\n\nPatient: I haven’t noticed anything like that, no. Not lip-smacking or picking at my clothes or my hands doing something over and over without me realizing it — at least not that I know of.\n\nWhen it happens, it feels less like I’m doing something automatically and more like I’m not doing anything, if that makes sense. I just sort of stop and stare, or I hold whatever I already had in my hand, and I’m trying to figure out what the next step is supposed to be. I don’t have a memory afterward of, like, finding out I’d been fiddling with something or repeating a motion.\n\nI guess it’s possible I might shift my hands or grip something tighter because I’m nervous, but I wouldn’t call that automatic in the way you’re describing. Nobody has pointed anything out to me either, like, “You kept smacking your lips,” or “You were fumbling with your hands.” Mostly what I’m aware of is the freezing and the panic of not being able to pull the routine back into my head.\n\nDoctor: After one of these blank episodes, do you ever feel confused or out of it for a little while afterward, or maybe feel like you're not fully back to normal right away?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do feel not quite right afterward, but I don’t know if I’d call it confused exactly. It’s more like my thinking is still slow, like the blankness doesn’t just switch off the second I realize what happened.\n\nI’ll be standing there with the road-closure forms in front of me, or holding the stop-slow paddle, and once I sort of “come back” to what I’m supposed to be doing, I’m still trying to piece it together. Like, okay, what was the next step, what did I already do, what am I supposed to do now? And that’s the part that scares me, because these are things I’ve done for years. I shouldn’t have to reconstruct them like they’re brand new instructions.\n\nSo afterward I might look normal from the outside, but inside I’m still kind of scrambling. My mind keeps going blank at the worst times, and even after the moment passes, I don’t feel fully steady right away. It’s like I need a little time to trust that my brain has caught up again.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with seizures or epilepsy, or has anyone ever told you that you looked like you were having a seizure?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never been diagnosed with seizures or epilepsy. Nobody has ever told me, like, “That looked like a seizure,” or anything like that.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I don’t really know what to make of these episodes. I hear “seizure” and I think of something more obvious, you know, like falling down or shaking, and that hasn’t been my experience. People have mostly just seemed to notice that I’m paused or not responding the way I should for a moment, but no one has said it looked like a seizure.\n\nI mean, I haven’t been evaluated specifically for that as far as I can say, so I don’t want to act like I know for sure what it is or isn’t. But no, no doctor has given me that diagnosis, and nobody around me has described it that way.\n\nDoctor: How often do these blank episodes happen? Do you notice them occurring regularly, like multiple times a week, or are they more occasional?\n\nPatient: It’s hard for me to give you a clean number, because I don’t always realize it right away until I’m already in the middle of it, if that makes sense. But it’s not just a one-time thing. It’s been happening enough that it’s why I came in.\n\nThe best way I can describe it is that my mind keeps going blank at the worst possible times. Like I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and suddenly I can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years. These are routines I should know automatically, and instead I’m just standing there trying to pull the sequence back into my head.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve tracked it like, “this many times per week,” because when it happens I’m mostly just trying to get through the moment without messing something up. But it feels regular enough that I’m worried about it. It’s not like an occasional forgetful moment where I walk into a room and forget why I’m there. It’s more serious than that, because it’s happening with work tasks I’ve done over and over, and it catches me off guard.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever noticed yourself doing things like lip-smacking, picking at your clothes, or fumbling with your hands without realizing it, especially during or right after these blank episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t noticed anything like that, at least not clearly.\n\nLike, nobody has said to me, “You were smacking your lips,” or “You were picking at your clothes,” or anything specific like that. And I don’t have a memory of doing it myself. During those moments I’m mostly aware of the blankness, or the feeling afterward where I’m trying to get my thoughts lined back up, so if my hands were doing something small, I honestly might not catch it.\n\nI do fidget sometimes just as a nervous thing, like anyone might, but I can’t say I’ve noticed it happening during those episodes in a way that stands out. I don’t want to say no like I’m absolutely certain, because part of the problem is I’m not fully tracking everything in the moment. But no, I haven’t specifically noticed lip-smacking or picking at my clothes or automatic hand movements like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel a warning or strange sensation coming before one of these blank episodes, like a feeling in your stomach, a strange smell, or something else unusual that happens right before it starts?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve tried to think about that because it would almost be helpful if there was some kind of warning, but I haven’t noticed one.\n\nI don’t get, like, a smell or a taste or some clear sensation in my stomach right before it happens. At least not that I can identify. It’s more like I’m doing what I’m supposed to be doing, and then suddenly I realize my mind isn’t catching the next step. There isn’t really a build-up that I can point to.\n\nSometimes afterward I’ll think, “Was there something right before that?” but I can’t find anything consistent. If there is a warning, it’s not obvious to me. It just kind of drops in, and then I’m stuck trying to get my thoughts back in order.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a time when someone else told you that you seemed completely out of it, not responding, or acting like you weren’t aware of what was happening around you?\n\nPatient: Not exactly in the way you’re saying, like I don’t think anyone has told me I was completely unreachable or not responding at all. I mean, I’ve tried to think about that because it would almost be helpful if there was some kind of warning, but I haven’t noticed one.\n\nI don’t get, like, a smell or a taste or some clear sensation in my stomach right before it happens. At least not that I can identify. It’s more like I’m doing what I’m supposed to be doing, and then suddenly I realize my mind isn’t catching the next step.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure. I wouldn’t say there’s a clear “crash” afterward, like I suddenly need to sleep or I feel like I’ve been drained in a dramatic way. At least I haven’t noticed it like that.

After it happens, I’m usually more… rattled, I guess. Like I’m trying to get myself back together and figure out what I missed or what I’m supposed to be doing next. That can make me feel worn out, but I don’t know if that’s tired in the way you mean, or if it’s more just stress from realizing my brain blanked on me again.

I don’t usually think, “Oh, now I’m sleepy because of that episode.” It’s more like I feel embarrassed or unsettled, and then I’m trying really hard to act normal and keep going. So, no, I haven’t clearly noticed unusual tiredness or sleepiness afterward. If it’s happening, it’s not obvious enough that I’ve connected it to those moments.

Can you tell me what’s been troubling you or what brings you here today?\n\nPatient: I came in because my mind keeps… going blank, I guess is the best way to put it. It’s not just forgetting little things like where I put my keys. It happens at the worst times, when I’m supposed to know exactly what I’m doing.\n\nLike with work, I’ll be looking right at the road-closure forms, or holding the stop-slow paddle, something I’ve done for years, and suddenly it’s like the steps disappear out of my head. I know I should know what comes next, and I’m standing there trying to pull it back, but there’s just this blank space. It scares me because those are not things you can be fuzzy about, you know? There are cars, people, instructions, routines… and I’ve done them so many times, but then my brain just doesn’t give me the next step.\n\nThat’s really what pushed me to come in. It feels like my thinking is not working when I need it most.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any other symptoms along with these blank episodes, like numbness, weakness, trouble speaking, or seeing something unusual like flashing lights or blind spots?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way I could clearly point to. I mean, when it happens I get really scared and kind of frozen because I’m trying so hard to remember what I’m supposed to do next, but I haven’t noticed, like, one side of my body going numb or weak or anything like that.\n\nTrouble speaking… I don’t know. Sometimes I probably get quiet because I’m embarrassed or panicking internally, but I don’t think my mouth stops working exactly. It’s more like my thoughts won’t line up fast enough for me to say anything useful.\n\nAnd seeing things like flashing lights or blind spots—no, I haven’t noticed that. Not that I can remember. It’s more just this blankness, like the instructions or the next step vanish, and then I’m standing there trying to act normal while my brain catches up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you briefly lost awareness of what was going on around you during one of these blank episodes, even for just a few seconds?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. That’s the hard part, because from inside it doesn’t feel like I black out exactly. I don’t come to and find myself somewhere else, or have someone telling me I was gone for a while. It’s more like… I’m there, I can see what’s in front of me, but I’m not taking it in the way I should.\n\nLike I might be staring at the sign or the paperwork or the road, and for a few seconds everything feels sort of paused in my head. I know people are around, cars are there, things are still happening, but my brain isn’t connecting the meaning of it fast enough. So I don’t know if that counts as losing awareness. It doesn’t feel like being unconscious. It feels more like I’m present but not really processing, if that makes sense.\n\nI haven’t noticed a clear gap where time is missing, but I also worry that maybe I wouldn’t know if it was only a few seconds.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had trouble remembering what happened right before or after one of these blank episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it trouble remembering what happened right before or right after, exactly. It’s more like the part I can’t remember is what I’m supposed to be doing in the moment.\n\nLike, I can usually tell you, “I was looking at the road-closure forms,” or “I had the stop-slow paddle in my hand,” or “I was standing there getting ready to do the next part.” That part is still there. But then the actual steps — the routine I’ve done for years — just drop out of my head. It’s like I’m staring right at the thing that should remind me, and it still doesn’t connect.\n\nAfterward, I can remember that it happened, and I remember feeling scared and embarrassed because I know I should know what comes next. But in the middle of it, it’s just blank. Not like a missing chunk of time so much as my mind not giving me the instructions I need.\n\nDoctor: During these blank episodes, has there ever been a moment where you felt like you couldn’t respond or move, even though you were aware of what was going on around you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, but it’s hard to describe because it doesn’t feel like my body is paralyzed or anything. It’s more like my mind just stops giving me the next instruction, and then I freeze because I don’t know what to do.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in, because it keeps happening at the worst possible times. I’ll be looking right at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and these are things I’ve done for years, things I should know without even thinking. And then suddenly it’s just… gone. The steps aren’t there. I know I’m standing there, I know the road is there, I know I’m supposed to be doing something, but I can’t pull the thought together fast enough to respond normally.\n\nSo if someone asked me something in that moment, I might not answer right away, not because I don’t hear them, but because I’m trying to make my brain catch up. And moving is kind of the same — I can move, I think, but I hesitate or just stand there because I don’t trust what the next move is supposed to be. It scares me because it’s not some complicated new thing. It’s basic steps I’ve done over and over, and then my mind just blanks out on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any shaking, jerking, or other movements that you couldn’t control during or right after one of these blank episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, like jerking or shaking that I couldn’t stop. I haven’t noticed my arms or legs doing anything on their own, and nobody has told me, “Hey, you were shaking,” or anything like that.\n\nWhen it happens, from what I can tell, I’m more just stuck or frozen for a moment, like I’m standing there trying to get my brain to catch up. Afterward I might feel rattled or embarrassed, but I can’t say I’ve had any obvious movements I couldn’t control. If there was something really subtle, I might not know, but nothing that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: During these blank episodes, have you ever noticed yourself making any automatic or repetitive movements, like lip-smacking, fumbling with your hands, or doing something over and over without realizing it?\n\nPatient: I haven’t noticed anything like that, no. Not lip-smacking or picking at my clothes or my hands doing something over and over without me realizing it — at least not that I know of.\n\nWhen it happens, it feels less like I’m doing something automatically and more like I’m not doing anything, if that makes sense. I just sort of stop and stare, or I hold whatever I already had in my hand, and I’m trying to figure out what the next step is supposed to be. I don’t have a memory afterward of, like, finding out I’d been fiddling with something or repeating a motion.\n\nI guess it’s possible I might shift my hands or grip something tighter because I’m nervous, but I wouldn’t call that automatic in the way you’re describing. Nobody has pointed anything out to me either, like, “You kept smacking your lips,” or “You were fumbling with your hands.” Mostly what I’m aware of is the freezing and the panic of not being able to pull the routine back into my head.\n\nDoctor: After one of these blank episodes, do you ever feel confused or out of it for a little while afterward, or maybe feel like you're not fully back to normal right away?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do feel not quite right afterward, but I don’t know if I’d call it confused exactly. It’s more like my thinking is still slow, like the blankness doesn’t just switch off the second I realize what happened.\n\nI’ll be standing there with the road-closure forms in front of me, or holding the stop-slow paddle, and once I sort of “come back” to what I’m supposed to be doing, I’m still trying to piece it together. Like, okay, what was the next step, what did I already do, what am I supposed to do now? And that’s the part that scares me, because these are things I’ve done for years. I shouldn’t have to reconstruct them like they’re brand new instructions.\n\nSo afterward I might look normal from the outside, but inside I’m still kind of scrambling. My mind keeps going blank at the worst times, and even after the moment passes, I don’t feel fully steady right away. It’s like I need a little time to trust that my brain has caught up again.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with seizures or epilepsy, or has anyone ever told you that you looked like you were having a seizure?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never been diagnosed with seizures or epilepsy. Nobody has ever told me, like, “That looked like a seizure,” or anything like that.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I don’t really know what to make of these episodes. I hear “seizure” and I think of something more obvious, you know, like falling down or shaking, and that hasn’t been my experience. People have mostly just seemed to notice that I’m paused or not responding the way I should for a moment, but no one has said it looked like a seizure.\n\nI mean, I haven’t been evaluated specifically for that as far as I can say, so I don’t want to act like I know for sure what it is or isn’t. But no, no doctor has given me that diagnosis, and nobody around me has described it that way.\n\nDoctor: How often do these blank episodes happen? Do you notice them occurring regularly, like multiple times a week, or are they more occasional?\n\nPatient: It’s hard for me to give you a clean number, because I don’t always realize it right away until I’m already in the middle of it, if that makes sense. But it’s not just a one-time thing. It’s been happening enough that it’s why I came in.\n\nThe best way I can describe it is that my mind keeps going blank at the worst possible times. Like I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and suddenly I can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years. These are routines I should know automatically, and instead I’m just standing there trying to pull the sequence back into my head.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve tracked it like, “this many times per week,” because when it happens I’m mostly just trying to get through the moment without messing something up. But it feels regular enough that I’m worried about it. It’s not like an occasional forgetful moment where I walk into a room and forget why I’m there. It’s more serious than that, because it’s happening with work tasks I’ve done over and over, and it catches me off guard.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever noticed yourself doing things like lip-smacking, picking at your clothes, or fumbling with your hands without realizing it, especially during or right after these blank episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t noticed anything like that, at least not clearly.\n\nLike, nobody has said to me, “You were smacking your lips,” or “You were picking at your clothes,” or anything specific like that. And then suddenly it’s just… gone. The steps aren’t there. I know I’m standing there, I know the road is there, I know I’m supposed to be doing something, but I can’t pull the thought together fast enough to respond normally.\n\nSo if someone asked me something in that moment, I might not answer right away, not because I don’t hear them, but because I’m trying to make my brain catch up. And moving is kind of the same — I can move, I think, but I hesitate or just stand there because I don’t trust what the next move is supposed to be. It scares me because it’s not some complicated new thing. It’s basic steps I’ve done over and over, and then my mind just blanks out on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any shaking, jerking, or other movements that you couldn’t control during or right after one of these blank episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, like jerking or shaking that I couldn’t stop. I haven’t noticed my arms or legs doing anything on their own, and nobody has told me, “Hey, you were shaking,” or anything like that.\n\nWhen it happens, from what I can tell, I’m more just stuck or frozen for a moment, like I’m standing there trying to get my brain to catch up. Afterward I might feel rattled or embarrassed, but I can’t say I’ve had any obvious movements I couldn’t control. If there was something really subtle, I might not know, but nothing that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: During these blank episodes, have you ever noticed yourself making any automatic or repetitive movements, like lip-smacking, fumbling with your hands, or doing something over and over without realizing it?\n\nPatient: I haven’t noticed anything like that, no. Not lip-smacking or picking at my clothes or my hands doing something over and over without me realizing it — at least not that I know of.\n\nWhen it happens, it feels less like I’m doing something automatically and more like I’m not doing anything, if that makes sense. I just sort of stop and stare, or I hold whatever I already had in my hand, and I’m trying to figure out what the next step is supposed to be. I don’t have a memory afterward of, like, finding out I’d been fiddling with something or repeating a motion.\n\nI guess it’s possible I might shift my hands or grip something tighter because I’m nervous, but I wouldn’t call that automatic in the way you’re describing. Nobody has pointed anything out to me either, like, “You kept smacking your lips,” or “You were fumbling with your hands.” Mostly what I’m aware of is the freezing and the panic of not being able to pull the routine back into my head.\n\nDoctor: After one of these blank episodes, do you ever feel confused or out of it for a little while afterward, or maybe feel like you're not fully back to normal right away?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do feel not quite right afterward, but I don’t know if I’d call it confused exactly. It’s more like my thinking is still slow, like the blankness doesn’t just switch off the second I realize what happened.\n\nI’ll be standing there with the road-closure forms in front of me, or holding the stop-slow paddle, and once I sort of “come back” to what I’m supposed to be doing, I’m still trying to piece it together. Like, okay, what was the next step, what did I already do, what am I supposed to do now? And that’s the part that scares me, because these are things I’ve done for years. I shouldn’t have to reconstruct them like they’re brand new instructions.\n\nSo afterward I might look normal from the outside, but inside I’m still kind of scrambling. My mind keeps going blank at the worst times, and even after the moment passes, I don’t feel fully steady right away. It’s like I need a little time to trust that my brain has caught up again.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with seizures or epilepsy, or has anyone ever told you that you looked like you were having a seizure?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never been diagnosed with seizures or epilepsy. Nobody has ever told me, like, “That looked like a seizure,” or anything like that.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I don’t really know what to make of these episodes. I hear “seizure” and I think of something more obvious, you know, like falling down or shaking, and that hasn’t been my experience. People have mostly just seemed to notice that I’m paused or not responding the way I should for a moment, but no one has said it looked like a seizure.\n\nI mean, I haven’t been evaluated specifically for that as far as I can say, so I don’t want to act like I know for sure what it is or isn’t. But no, no doctor has given me that diagnosis, and nobody around me has described it that way.\n\nDoctor: How often do these blank episodes happen? Do you notice them occurring regularly, like multiple times a week, or are they more occasional?\n\nPatient: It’s hard for me to give you a clean number, because I don’t always realize it right away until I’m already in the middle of it, if that makes sense. But it’s not just a one-time thing. It’s been happening enough that it’s why I came in.\n\nThe best way I can describe it is that my mind keeps going blank at the worst possible times. Like I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and suddenly I can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years. These are routines I should know automatically, and instead I’m just standing there trying to pull the sequence back into my head.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve tracked it like, “this many times per week,” because when it happens I’m mostly just trying to get through the moment without messing something up. But it feels regular enough that I’m worried about it. It’s not like an occasional forgetful moment where I walk into a room and forget why I’m there. It’s more serious than that, because it’s happening with work tasks I’ve done over and over, and it catches me off guard.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever noticed yourself doing things like lip-smacking, picking at your clothes, or fumbling with your hands without realizing it, especially during or right after these blank episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t noticed anything like that, at least not clearly.\n\nLike, nobody has said to me, “You were smacking your lips,” or “You were picking at your clothes,” or anything specific like that. And I don’t have a memory of doing it myself. During those moments I’m mostly aware of the blankness, or the feeling afterward where I’m trying to get my thoughts lined back up, so if my hands were doing something small, I honestly might not catch it.\n\nI do fidget sometimes just as a nervous thing, like anyone might, but I can’t say I’ve noticed it happening during those episodes in a way that stands out. I don’t want to say no like I’m absolutely certain, because part of the problem is I’m not fully tracking everything in the moment. But no, I haven’t specifically noticed lip-smacking or picking at my clothes or automatic hand movements like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel a warning or strange sensation coming before one of these blank episodes, like a feeling in your stomach, a strange smell, or something else unusual that happens right before it starts?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve tried to think about that because it would almost be helpful if there was some kind of warning, but I haven’t noticed one.\n\nI don’t get, like, a smell or a taste or some clear sensation in my stomach right before it happens. At least not that I can identify. It’s more like I’m doing what I’m supposed to be doing, and then suddenly I realize my mind isn’t catching the next step. There isn’t really a build-up that I can point to.\n\nSometimes afterward I’ll think, “Was there something right before that?” but I can’t find anything consistent. If there is a warning, it’s not obvious to me. It just kind of drops in, and then I’m stuck trying to get my thoughts back in order.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a time when someone else told you that you seemed completely out of it, not responding, or acting like you weren’t aware of what was happening around you?\n\nPatient: Not exactly in the way you’re saying, like I don’t think anyone has told me I was completely unreachable or not responding at all. But people have noticed that I sort of freeze or get stuck, especially at work, and that’s part of what scares me.\n\nIt’s like I’ll be standing there with the road-closure forms, or holding the stop-slow paddle, and then suddenly the steps just disappear from my head. These are things I’ve done for years, so I should know them without even thinking, but in that moment I’m just blank. I’m aware enough to know something is wrong, but I can’t pull the next part forward.\n\nI don’t know if from the outside it looks like I’m “out of it” or just confused or slow. I’ve had moments where I can tell someone is waiting on me, or I’m trying to act normal because I don’t want to draw attention, but inside I’m panicking because my mind has gone empty. So, yes, people may have seen me looking stuck or not fully there, but I can’t say anyone has described it to me exactly as “you weren’t aware of what was happening.” It’s more that I’m there, but my thinking just drops out on me.\n\nDoctor: After one of these blank episodes, do you ever feel unusually tired or sleepy, like you just don’t have the same energy as before it happened?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I wouldn’t say there’s a clear “crash” afterward, like I suddenly need to sleep or I feel like I’ve been drained in a dramatic way. It feels like my thinking is not working when I need it most.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any other symptoms along with these blank episodes, like numbness, weakness, trouble speaking, or seeing something unusual like flashing lights or blind spots?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way I could clearly point to. I mean, when it happens I get really scared and kind of frozen because I’m trying so hard to remember what I’m supposed to do next, but I haven’t noticed, like, one side of my body going numb or weak or anything like that.\n\nTrouble speaking… I don’t know. Sometimes I probably get quiet because I’m embarrassed or panicking internally, but I don’t think my mouth stops working exactly. It’s more like my thoughts won’t line up fast enough for me to say anything useful.\n\nAnd seeing things like flashing lights or blind spots—no, I haven’t noticed that. Not that I can remember. It’s more just this blankness, like the instructions or the next step vanish, and then I’m standing there trying to act normal while my brain catches up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you briefly lost awareness of what was going on around you during one of these blank episodes, even for just a few seconds?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. That’s the hard part, because from inside it doesn’t feel like I black out exactly. I don’t come to and find myself somewhere else, or have someone telling me I was gone for a while. It’s more like… I’m there, I can see what’s in front of me, but I’m not taking it in the way I should.\n\nLike I might be staring at the sign or the paperwork or the road, and for a few seconds everything feels sort of paused in my head. I know people are around, cars are there, things are still happening, but my brain isn’t connecting the meaning of it fast enough. So I don’t know if that counts as losing awareness. It doesn’t feel like being unconscious. It feels more like I’m present but not really processing, if that makes sense.\n\nI haven’t noticed a clear gap where time is missing, but I also worry that maybe I wouldn’t know if it was only a few seconds.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had trouble remembering what happened right before or after one of these blank episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it trouble remembering what happened right before or right after, exactly. It’s more like the part I can’t remember is what I’m supposed to be doing in the moment.\n\nLike, I can usually tell you, “I was looking at the road-closure forms,” or “I had the stop-slow paddle in my hand,” or “I was standing there getting ready to do the next part.” That part is still there. But then the actual steps — the routine I’ve done for years — just drop out of my head. It’s like I’m staring right at the thing that should remind me, and it still doesn’t connect.\n\nAfterward, I can remember that it happened, and I remember feeling scared and embarrassed because I know I should know what comes next. But in the middle of it, it’s just blank. Not like a missing chunk of time so much as my mind not giving me the instructions I need.\n\nDoctor: During these blank episodes, has there ever been a moment where you felt like you couldn’t respond or move, even though you were aware of what was going on around you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, but it’s hard to describe because it doesn’t feel like my body is paralyzed or anything. It’s more like my mind just stops giving me the next instruction, and then I freeze because I don’t know what to do.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in, because it keeps happening at the worst possible times. I’ll be looking right at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and these are things I’ve done for years, things I should know without even thinking. And then suddenly it’s just… gone. The steps aren’t there. I know I’m standing there, I know the road is there, I know I’m supposed to be doing something, but I can’t pull the thought together fast enough to respond normally.\n\nSo if someone asked me something in that moment, I might not answer right away, not because I don’t hear them, but because I’m trying to make my brain catch up. And moving is kind of the same — I can move, I think, but I hesitate or just stand there because I don’t trust what the next move is supposed to be. It scares me because it’s not some complicated new thing. It’s basic steps I’ve done over and over, and then my mind just blanks out on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any shaking, jerking, or other movements that you couldn’t control during or right after one of these blank episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, like jerking or shaking that I couldn’t stop. I haven’t noticed my arms or legs doing anything on their own, and nobody has told me, “Hey, you were shaking,” or anything like that.\n\nWhen it happens, from what I can tell, I’m more just stuck or frozen for a moment, like I’m standing there trying to get my brain to catch up. Afterward I might feel rattled or embarrassed, but I can’t say I’ve had any obvious movements I couldn’t control. If there was something really subtle, I might not know, but nothing that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: During these blank episodes, have you ever noticed yourself making any automatic or repetitive movements, like lip-smacking, fumbling with your hands, or doing something over and over without realizing it?\n\nPatient: I haven’t noticed anything like that, no. Not lip-smacking or picking at my clothes or my hands doing something over and over without me realizing it — at least not that I know of.\n\nWhen it happens, it feels less like I’m doing something automatically and more like I’m not doing anything, if that makes sense. I just sort of stop and stare, or I hold whatever I already had in my hand, and I’m trying to figure out what the next step is supposed to be. I don’t have a memory afterward of, like, finding out I’d been fiddling with something or repeating a motion.\n\nI guess it’s possible I might shift my hands or grip something tighter because I’m nervous, but I wouldn’t call that automatic in the way you’re describing. Nobody has pointed anything out to me either, like, “You kept smacking your lips,” or “You were fumbling with your hands.” Mostly what I’m aware of is the freezing and the panic of not being able to pull the routine back into my head.\n\nDoctor: After one of these blank episodes, do you ever feel confused or out of it for a little while afterward, or maybe feel like you're not fully back to normal right away?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do feel not quite right afterward, but I don’t know if I’d call it confused exactly. It’s more like my thinking is still slow, like the blankness doesn’t just switch off the second I realize what happened.\n\nI’ll be standing there with the road-closure forms in front of me, or holding the stop-slow paddle, and once I sort of “come back” to what I’m supposed to be doing, I’m still trying to piece it together. Like, okay, what was the next step, what did I already do, what am I supposed to do now? And that’s the part that scares me, because these are things I’ve done for years. I shouldn’t have to reconstruct them like they’re brand new instructions.\n\nSo afterward I might look normal from the outside, but inside I’m still kind of scrambling. My mind keeps going blank at the worst times, and even after the moment passes, I don’t feel fully steady right away. It’s like I need a little time to trust that my brain has caught up again.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with seizures or epilepsy, or has anyone ever told you that you looked like you were having a seizure?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never been diagnosed with seizures or epilepsy. Nobody has ever told me, like, “That looked like a seizure,” or anything like that.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I don’t really know what to make of these episodes. I hear “seizure” and I think of something more obvious, you know, like falling down or shaking, and that hasn’t been my experience. People have mostly just seemed to notice that I’m paused or not responding the way I should for a moment, but no one has said it looked like a seizure.\n\nI mean, I haven’t been evaluated specifically for that as far as I can say, so I don’t want to act like I know for sure what it is or isn’t. But no, no doctor has given me that diagnosis, and nobody around me has described it that way.\n\nDoctor: How often do these blank episodes happen? Do you notice them occurring regularly, like multiple times a week, or are they more occasional?\n\nPatient: It’s hard for me to give you a clean number, because I don’t always realize it right away until I’m already in the middle of it, if that makes sense. But it’s not just a one-time thing. It’s been happening enough that it’s why I came in.\n\nThe best way I can describe it is that my mind keeps going blank at the worst possible times. Like I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and suddenly I can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years. These are routines I should know automatically, and instead I’m just standing there trying to pull the sequence back into my head.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve tracked it like, “this many times per week,” because when it happens I’m mostly just trying to get through the moment without messing something up. But it feels regular enough that I’m worried about it. It’s not like an occasional forgetful moment where I walk into a room and forget why I’m there. It’s more serious than that, because it’s happening with work tasks I’ve done over and over, and it catches me off guard.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever noticed yourself doing things like lip-smacking, picking at your clothes, or fumbling with your hands without realizing it, especially during or right after these blank episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t noticed anything like that, at least not clearly.\n\nLike, nobody has said to me, “You were smacking your lips,” or “You were picking at your clothes,” or anything specific like that. And I don’t have a memory of doing it myself. During those moments I’m mostly aware of the blankness, or the feeling afterward where I’m trying to get my thoughts lined back up, so if my hands were doing something small, I honestly might not catch it.\n\nI do fidget sometimes just as a nervous thing, like anyone might, but I can’t say I’ve noticed it happening during those episodes in a way that stands out. I don’t want to say no like I’m absolutely certain, because part of the problem is I’m not fully tracking everything in the moment. But no, I haven’t specifically noticed lip-smacking or picking at my clothes or automatic hand movements like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel a warning or strange sensation coming before one of these blank episodes, like a feeling in your stomach, a strange smell, or something else unusual that happens right before it starts?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve tried to think about that because it would almost be helpful if there was some kind of warning, but I haven’t noticed one.\n\nI don’t get, like, a smell or a taste or some clear sensation in my stomach right before it happens. At least not that I can identify. It’s more like I’m doing what I’m supposed to be doing, and then suddenly I realize my mind isn’t catching the next step. There isn’t really a build-up that I can point to.\n\nSometimes afterward I’ll think, “Was there something right before that?” but I can’t find anything consistent. If there is a warning, it’s not obvious to me. It just kind of drops in, and then I’m stuck trying to get my thoughts back in order.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a time when someone else told you that you seemed completely out of it, not responding, or acting like you weren’t aware of what was happening around you?\n\nPatient: Not exactly in the way you’re saying, like I don’t think anyone has told me I was completely unreachable or not responding at all. But people have noticed that I sort of freeze or get stuck, especially at work, and that’s part of what scares me.\n\nIt’s like I’ll be standing there with the road-closure forms, or holding the stop-slow paddle, and then suddenly the steps just disappear from my head. These are things I’ve done for years, so I should know them without even thinking, but in that moment I’m just blank. I’m aware enough to know something is wrong, but I can’t pull the next part forward.\n\nI don’t know if from the outside it looks like I’m “out of it” or just confused or slow. I’ve had moments where I can tell someone is waiting on me, or I’m trying to act normal because I don’t want to draw attention, but inside I’m panicking because my mind has gone empty. So, yes, people may have seen me looking stuck or not fully there, but I can’t say anyone has described it to me exactly as “you weren’t aware of what was happening.” It’s more that I’m there, but my thinking just drops out on me.\n\nDoctor: After one of these blank episodes, do you ever feel unusually tired or sleepy, like you just don’t have the same energy as before it happened?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I wouldn’t say there’s a clear “crash” afterward, like I suddenly need to sleep or I feel like I’ve been drained in a dramatic way. At least I haven’t noticed it like that.\n\nAfter it happens, I’m usually more… rattled, I guess. Like I’m trying to get myself back together and figure out what I missed or what I’m supposed to be doing next. That can make me feel worn out, but I don’t know if that’s tired in the way you mean, or if it’s more just stress from realizing my brain blanked on me again.\n\nI don’t usually think, “Oh, now I’m sleepy because of that episode.” It’s more like I feel embarrassed or unsettled, and then I’m trying really hard to act normal and keep going. So, no, I haven’t clearly noticed unusual tiredness or sleepiness afterward. If it’s happening, it’s not obvious enough that I’ve connected it to those moments.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any head injuries, concussions, or significant trauma to your head that might be related to these episodes?\n\nPatient: Not that I can think of, no. I mean, I’ve tried to run through that in my head because I know people ask about concussions and head injuries with things like this, but I don’t have some obvious incident that stands out.\n\nI don’t remember ever being told I had a concussion or having, like, a serious hit to the head where afterward everyone was worried or I had to be treated for it. Nothing like a car accident or a fall where I hit my head badly, at least not that I can recall.\n\nI guess it’s possible I’ve bumped my head here or there like anybody does, but nothing significant that I would connect to these blank moments. So my answer would be no, not that I know of.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Yes… I think that’s probably the main reason I came in, honestly. It’s not just normal distraction, at least it doesn’t feel normal to me. My mind keeps going blank at the worst possible times.

Like I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and suddenly I can’t remember the steps. And that’s the part that really bothers me, because these are things I’ve done for years. I should be able to do them automatically. I shouldn’t have to stand there searching my brain for what comes next.

It feels like everything just drops out for a moment. I’m still there, I know I’m there, but the thinking part just… disappears. And then I get stressed because I know I need to keep moving, cars are there, people are expecting me to know what I’m doing, and I’m trying not to look like I’m lost. But inside I’m scrambling.

So yes, stress definitely makes it harder, and once I notice it happening I get even more anxious, because then I’m thinking, “What if I mess this up? What if I can’t remember the next step?” It kind of feeds on itself. But the blankness is the thing that scares me most. That’s really what brought me here.

It doesn’t feel like being unconscious. Not like a missing chunk of time so much as my mind not giving me the instructions I need.\n\nDoctor: During these blank episodes, has there ever been a moment where you felt like you couldn’t respond or move, even though you were aware of what was going on around you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, but it’s hard to describe because it doesn’t feel like my body is paralyzed or anything. It’s more like my mind just stops giving me the next instruction, and then I freeze because I don’t know what to do.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in, because it keeps happening at the worst possible times. And then suddenly it’s just… gone. The steps aren’t there. I know I’m standing there, I know the road is there, I know I’m supposed to be doing something, but I can’t pull the thought together fast enough to respond normally.\n\nSo if someone asked me something in that moment, I might not answer right away, not because I don’t hear them, but because I’m trying to make my brain catch up. And moving is kind of the same — I can move, I think, but I hesitate or just stand there because I don’t trust what the next move is supposed to be. It scares me because it’s not some complicated new thing. It’s basic steps I’ve done over and over, and then my mind just blanks out on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any shaking, jerking, or other movements that you couldn’t control during or right after one of these blank episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, like jerking or shaking that I couldn’t stop. I haven’t noticed my arms or legs doing anything on their own, and nobody has told me, “Hey, you were shaking,” or anything like that.\n\nWhen it happens, from what I can tell, I’m more just stuck or frozen for a moment, like I’m standing there trying to get my brain to catch up. Afterward I might feel rattled or embarrassed, but I can’t say I’ve had any obvious movements I couldn’t control. If there was something really subtle, I might not know, but nothing that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: During these blank episodes, have you ever noticed yourself making any automatic or repetitive movements, like lip-smacking, fumbling with your hands, or doing something over and over without realizing it?\n\nPatient: I haven’t noticed anything like that, no. Not lip-smacking or picking at my clothes or my hands doing something over and over without me realizing it — at least not that I know of.\n\nWhen it happens, it feels less like I’m doing something automatically and more like I’m not doing anything, if that makes sense. I just sort of stop and stare, or I hold whatever I already had in my hand, and I’m trying to figure out what the next step is supposed to be. I don’t have a memory afterward of, like, finding out I’d been fiddling with something or repeating a motion.\n\nI guess it’s possible I might shift my hands or grip something tighter because I’m nervous, but I wouldn’t call that automatic in the way you’re describing. Nobody has pointed anything out to me either, like, “You kept smacking your lips,” or “You were fumbling with your hands.” Mostly what I’m aware of is the freezing and the panic of not being able to pull the routine back into my head.\n\nDoctor: After one of these blank episodes, do you ever feel confused or out of it for a little while afterward, or maybe feel like you're not fully back to normal right away?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do feel not quite right afterward, but I don’t know if I’d call it confused exactly. It’s more like my thinking is still slow, like the blankness doesn’t just switch off the second I realize what happened.\n\nI’ll be standing there with the road-closure forms in front of me, or holding the stop-slow paddle, and once I sort of “come back” to what I’m supposed to be doing, I’m still trying to piece it together. Like, okay, what was the next step, what did I already do, what am I supposed to do now? And that’s the part that scares me, because these are things I’ve done for years. I shouldn’t have to reconstruct them like they’re brand new instructions.\n\nSo afterward I might look normal from the outside, but inside I’m still kind of scrambling. My mind keeps going blank at the worst times, and even after the moment passes, I don’t feel fully steady right away. It’s like I need a little time to trust that my brain has caught up again.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with seizures or epilepsy, or has anyone ever told you that you looked like you were having a seizure?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never been diagnosed with seizures or epilepsy. Nobody has ever told me, like, “That looked like a seizure,” or anything like that.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I don’t really know what to make of these episodes. I hear “seizure” and I think of something more obvious, you know, like falling down or shaking, and that hasn’t been my experience. People have mostly just seemed to notice that I’m paused or not responding the way I should for a moment, but no one has said it looked like a seizure.\n\nI mean, I haven’t been evaluated specifically for that as far as I can say, so I don’t want to act like I know for sure what it is or isn’t. But no, no doctor has given me that diagnosis, and nobody around me has described it that way.\n\nDoctor: How often do these blank episodes happen? Do you notice them occurring regularly, like multiple times a week, or are they more occasional?\n\nPatient: It’s hard for me to give you a clean number, because I don’t always realize it right away until I’m already in the middle of it, if that makes sense. But it’s not just a one-time thing. It’s been happening enough that it’s why I came in.\n\nThe best way I can describe it is that my mind keeps going blank at the worst possible times. These are routines I should know automatically, and instead I’m just standing there trying to pull the sequence back into my head.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve tracked it like, “this many times per week,” because when it happens I’m mostly just trying to get through the moment without messing something up. It’s more serious than that, because it’s happening with work tasks I’ve done over and over, and it catches me off guard.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever noticed yourself doing things like lip-smacking, picking at your clothes, or fumbling with your hands without realizing it, especially during or right after these blank episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t noticed anything like that, at least not clearly.\n\nLike, nobody has said to me, “You were smacking your lips,” or “You were picking at your clothes,” or anything specific like that. And I don’t have a memory of doing it myself. During those moments I’m mostly aware of the blankness, or the feeling afterward where I’m trying to get my thoughts lined back up, so if my hands were doing something small, I honestly might not catch it.\n\nI do fidget sometimes just as a nervous thing, like anyone might, but I can’t say I’ve noticed it happening during those episodes in a way that stands out. I don’t want to say no like I’m absolutely certain, because part of the problem is I’m not fully tracking everything in the moment. But no, I haven’t specifically noticed lip-smacking or picking at my clothes or automatic hand movements like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel a warning or strange sensation coming before one of these blank episodes, like a feeling in your stomach, a strange smell, or something else unusual that happens right before it starts?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I don’t think so… not that I’ve caught, anyway. I mean, I know what you mean by like, doing something automatically and not realizing it, but I haven’t had anyone tell me, “You were smacking your lips,” or “You kept picking at your clothes,” or anything like that.

No, I still don’t think so. I mean, I’ve been trying to pay attention since you asked it that way, because maybe I would miss something if I’m blanking, but I don’t have any clear memory of doing lip-smacking or fumbling around with my hands in a repetitive way.

It feels like my thinking is not working when I need it most.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any other symptoms along with these blank episodes, like numbness, weakness, trouble speaking, or seeing something unusual like flashing lights or blind spots?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way I could clearly point to. I mean, when it happens I get really scared and kind of frozen because I’m trying so hard to remember what I’m supposed to do next, but I haven’t noticed, like, one side of my body going numb or weak or anything like that.\n\nTrouble speaking… I don’t know. Sometimes I probably get quiet because I’m embarrassed or panicking internally, but I don’t think my mouth stops working exactly. It’s more like my thoughts won’t line up fast enough for me to say anything useful.\n\nAnd seeing things like flashing lights or blind spots—no, I haven’t noticed that. Not that I can remember. It’s more just this blankness, like the instructions or the next step vanish, and then I’m standing there trying to act normal while my brain catches up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you briefly lost awareness of what was going on around you during one of these blank episodes, even for just a few seconds?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. That’s the hard part, because from inside it doesn’t feel like I black out exactly. I don’t come to and find myself somewhere else, or have someone telling me I was gone for a while. It’s more like… I’m there, I can see what’s in front of me, but I’m not taking it in the way I should.\n\nLike I might be staring at the sign or the paperwork or the road, and for a few seconds everything feels sort of paused in my head. I know people are around, cars are there, things are still happening, but my brain isn’t connecting the meaning of it fast enough. So I don’t know if that counts as losing awareness. It doesn’t feel like being unconscious. It feels more like I’m present but not really processing, if that makes sense.\n\nI haven’t noticed a clear gap where time is missing, but I also worry that maybe I wouldn’t know if it was only a few seconds.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had trouble remembering what happened right before or after one of these blank episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it trouble remembering what happened right before or right after, exactly. It’s more like the part I can’t remember is what I’m supposed to be doing in the moment.\n\nLike, I can usually tell you, “I was looking at the road-closure forms,” or “I had the stop-slow paddle in my hand,” or “I was standing there getting ready to do the next part.” That part is still there. But then the actual steps — the routine I’ve done for years — just drop out of my head. It’s like I’m staring right at the thing that should remind me, and it still doesn’t connect.\n\nAfterward, I can remember that it happened, and I remember feeling scared and embarrassed because I know I should know what comes next. But in the middle of it, it’s just blank. Not like a missing chunk of time so much as my mind not giving me the instructions I need.\n\nDoctor: During these blank episodes, has there ever been a moment where you felt like you couldn’t respond or move, even though you were aware of what was going on around you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, but it’s hard to describe because it doesn’t feel like my body is paralyzed or anything. It’s more like my mind just stops giving me the next instruction, and then I freeze because I don’t know what to do.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in, because it keeps happening at the worst possible times. I’ll be looking right at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and these are things I’ve done for years, things I should know without even thinking. And then suddenly it’s just… gone. The steps aren’t there. I know I’m standing there, I know the road is there, I know I’m supposed to be doing something, but I can’t pull the thought together fast enough to respond normally.\n\nSo if someone asked me something in that moment, I might not answer right away, not because I don’t hear them, but because I’m trying to make my brain catch up. And moving is kind of the same — I can move, I think, but I hesitate or just stand there because I don’t trust what the next move is supposed to be. It scares me because it’s not some complicated new thing. It’s basic steps I’ve done over and over, and then my mind just blanks out on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any shaking, jerking, or other movements that you couldn’t control during or right after one of these blank episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, like jerking or shaking that I couldn’t stop. I haven’t noticed my arms or legs doing anything on their own, and nobody has told me, “Hey, you were shaking,” or anything like that.\n\nWhen it happens, from what I can tell, I’m more just stuck or frozen for a moment, like I’m standing there trying to get my brain to catch up. Afterward I might feel rattled or embarrassed, but I can’t say I’ve had any obvious movements I couldn’t control. If there was something really subtle, I might not know, but nothing that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: During these blank episodes, have you ever noticed yourself making any automatic or repetitive movements, like lip-smacking, fumbling with your hands, or doing something over and over without realizing it?\n\nPatient: I haven’t noticed anything like that, no. Not lip-smacking or picking at my clothes or my hands doing something over and over without me realizing it — at least not that I know of.\n\nWhen it happens, it feels less like I’m doing something automatically and more like I’m not doing anything, if that makes sense. I just sort of stop and stare, or I hold whatever I already had in my hand, and I’m trying to figure out what the next step is supposed to be. I don’t have a memory afterward of, like, finding out I’d been fiddling with something or repeating a motion.\n\nI guess it’s possible I might shift my hands or grip something tighter because I’m nervous, but I wouldn’t call that automatic in the way you’re describing. Nobody has pointed anything out to me either, like, “You kept smacking your lips,” or “You were fumbling with your hands.” Mostly what I’m aware of is the freezing and the panic of not being able to pull the routine back into my head.\n\nDoctor: After one of these blank episodes, do you ever feel confused or out of it for a little while afterward, or maybe feel like you're not fully back to normal right away?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do feel not quite right afterward, but I don’t know if I’d call it confused exactly. It’s more like my thinking is still slow, like the blankness doesn’t just switch off the second I realize what happened.\n\nI’ll be standing there with the road-closure forms in front of me, or holding the stop-slow paddle, and once I sort of “come back” to what I’m supposed to be doing, I’m still trying to piece it together. Like, okay, what was the next step, what did I already do, what am I supposed to do now? And that’s the part that scares me, because these are things I’ve done for years. I shouldn’t have to reconstruct them like they’re brand new instructions.\n\nSo afterward I might look normal from the outside, but inside I’m still kind of scrambling. My mind keeps going blank at the worst times, and even after the moment passes, I don’t feel fully steady right away. It’s like I need a little time to trust that my brain has caught up again.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with seizures or epilepsy, or has anyone ever told you that you looked like you were having a seizure?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never been diagnosed with seizures or epilepsy. Nobody has ever told me, like, “That looked like a seizure,” or anything like that.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I don’t really know what to make of these episodes. I hear “seizure” and I think of something more obvious, you know, like falling down or shaking, and that hasn’t been my experience. People have mostly just seemed to notice that I’m paused or not responding the way I should for a moment, but no one has said it looked like a seizure.\n\nI mean, I haven’t been evaluated specifically for that as far as I can say, so I don’t want to act like I know for sure what it is or isn’t. But no, no doctor has given me that diagnosis, and nobody around me has described it that way.\n\nDoctor: How often do these blank episodes happen? Do you notice them occurring regularly, like multiple times a week, or are they more occasional?\n\nPatient: It’s hard for me to give you a clean number, because I don’t always realize it right away until I’m already in the middle of it, if that makes sense. But it’s not just a one-time thing. It’s been happening enough that it’s why I came in.\n\nThe best way I can describe it is that my mind keeps going blank at the worst possible times. Like I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and suddenly I can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years. These are routines I should know automatically, and instead I’m just standing there trying to pull the sequence back into my head.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve tracked it like, “this many times per week,” because when it happens I’m mostly just trying to get through the moment without messing something up. But it feels regular enough that I’m worried about it. It’s not like an occasional forgetful moment where I walk into a room and forget why I’m there. It’s more serious than that, because it’s happening with work tasks I’ve done over and over, and it catches me off guard.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever noticed yourself doing things like lip-smacking, picking at your clothes, or fumbling with your hands without realizing it, especially during or right after these blank episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t noticed anything like that, at least not clearly.\n\nLike, nobody has said to me, “You were smacking your lips,” or “You were picking at your clothes,” or anything specific like that. And I don’t have a memory of doing it myself. During those moments I’m mostly aware of the blankness, or the feeling afterward where I’m trying to get my thoughts lined back up, so if my hands were doing something small, I honestly might not catch it.\n\nI do fidget sometimes just as a nervous thing, like anyone might, but I can’t say I’ve noticed it happening during those episodes in a way that stands out. I don’t want to say no like I’m absolutely certain, because part of the problem is I’m not fully tracking everything in the moment. But no, I haven’t specifically noticed lip-smacking or picking at my clothes or automatic hand movements like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel a warning or strange sensation coming before one of these blank episodes, like a feeling in your stomach, a strange smell, or something else unusual that happens right before it starts?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve tried to think about that because it would almost be helpful if there was some kind of warning, but I haven’t noticed one.\n\nI don’t get, like, a smell or a taste or some clear sensation in my stomach right before it happens. At least not that I can identify. It’s more like I’m doing what I’m supposed to be doing, and then suddenly I realize my mind isn’t catching the next step. There isn’t really a build-up that I can point to.\n\nSometimes afterward I’ll think, “Was there something right before that?” but I can’t find anything consistent. If there is a warning, it’s not obvious to me. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if anyone has said it exactly like that, like “you were staring off into space.” What I can say is, the reason I came in is because my mind keeps going blank at really bad times, and it probably looks strange from the outside.

Like I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or standing there with the stop-slow paddle, and then suddenly I can’t remember the steps. And these are things I’ve done for years, so it’s not like I’m new and confused. It’s more like everything in my head just stops for a moment, and I’m trying to get it back.

So I’m not sure if other people would describe me as dazed or unresponsive. I haven’t really asked them. But I do feel stuck in those moments, like I’m there physically but my thinking just blanks out, and that’s what scares me.
